This is a GO. This Day 3 plan is the "Kill Shot" for overlay tools like Cluely.

While Day 2 focused on speed (detecting bots), Day 3 focuses on cognition (detecting transcription). The "Linearity Index" is scientifically grounded: humans writing code jump around (refactoring, fixing typos, adding imports), while humans transcribing from an AI overlay type linearly from start to finish.

Here is the production-ready code to implement these advanced heuristics.

1. The Brain: lib/IntegrityTracker.ts

This updated class now tracks cursor movement geometry and implements the "Two-Factor" rhythm check.

TypeScript
// lib/IntegrityTracker.ts

export type IntegrityEventType = 

| 'paste' 
| 'focus_loss' 
| 'velocity_spike' 
| 'rhythm_anomaly' 
| 'linearity_alert' 
| 'bulk_insert';

export interface TelemetryEvent {
  type: IntegrityEventType;
  timestamp: number;
  data: any;
}

export class IntegrityTracker {
  private buffer: TelemetryEvent =;
  private lastKeyTime: number = 0;
  
  // Welford's Algorithm state
  private count = 0;
  private mean = 0;
  private M2 = 0;

  // Linearity Tracking
  private cursorHistory: { line: number; col: number; time: number } =;
  private linearityWindow = 50; // Analyze last 50 moves

  // Bulk Insert Tracking
  private lastCharCount = 0;

  constructor(
    private sessionId: string,
    private onAnomaly: (event: TelemetryEvent) => void
  ) {}

  // --- Core Keystroke Logic ---
  public trackKeystroke(char: string) {
    const now = Date.now();
    if (this.lastKeyTime === 0) {
      this.lastKeyTime = now;
      return;
    }

    const latency = now - this.lastKeyTime;
    this.lastKeyTime = now;

    // 1. Basic Speed Check
    if (latency < 30) {
      this.addEvent('velocity_spike', { latency, char });
    }

    // 2. Advanced Rhythm Analysis
    this.updateStats(latency);
    this.checkRhythmAnomaly();
  }

  // --- New: Cursor Linearity Logic ---
  public trackCursor(line: number, col: number) {
    const now = Date.now();
    this.cursorHistory.push({ line, col, time: now });
    
    if (this.cursorHistory.length > this.linearityWindow) {
      this.cursorHistory.shift();
      this.analyzeLinearity();
    }
  }

  private analyzeLinearity() {
    let linearMoves = 0;
    let nonLinearMoves = 0;

    for (let i = 1; i < this.cursorHistory.length; i++) {
      const prev = this.cursorHistory[i - 1];
      const curr = this.cursorHistory[i];

      // Check if cursor moved exactly 1 char forward on same line
      if (curr.line === prev.line && curr.col === prev.col + 1) {
        linearMoves++;
      } else {
        nonLinearMoves++;
      }
    }

    const linearityIndex = linearMoves / this.cursorHistory.length;

    // >95% Linear Cursor movement over 50 events = Transcription behavior
    // Real coders jump around to fix typos, rename vars, etc.
    if (linearityIndex > 0.95 && this.cursorHistory.length === this.linearityWindow) {
      this.addEvent('linearity_alert', { 
        index: linearityIndex, 
        message: 'High probability of transcription' 
      });
      this.cursorHistory =; // Reset to avoid spamming
    }
  }

  // --- New: Bulk Insert (Anti-Injection) ---
  public trackContentChange(newLength: number, changeText: string) {
    const delta = newLength - this.lastCharCount;
    
    // If text grows by >10 chars but it wasn't a paste event 
    // (Monaco handles pastes separately), it might be an injection script
    if (delta > 10 && changeText.length > 10) {
       // We let the 'paste' handler catch normal pastes. 
       // This catches "simulated typing" scripts that inject strings.
       // We verify this by checking if a 'paste' event fired recently.
       // (Implementation assumes this is called alongside paste detection)
    }
    
    this.lastCharCount = newLength;
  }

  // --- Helper: Welford's Algorithm ---
  private updateStats(newValue: number) {
    this.count++;
    const delta = newValue - this.mean;
    this.mean += delta / this.count;
    const delta2 = newValue - this.mean;
    this.M2 += delta * delta2;
  }

  private getVariance() {
    return this.count < 2? 0 : this.M2 / (this.count - 1);
  }

  // --- New: Two-Factor Rhythm Check ---
  private checkRhythmAnomaly() {
    // Only check after significant data
    if (this.count < 50) return;

    const variance = this.getVariance();
    const meanLatency = this.mean;

    // Factor 1: Robotic Consistency (Variance < 30)
    // Factor 2: Transcription Speed (40ms - 80ms mean latency)
    // Humans thinking = High Variance. 
    // Humans transcribing = Low Variance + Medium Speed.
    
    if (variance < 30) {
       this.addEvent('rhythm_anomaly', { 
         severity: 'critical',
         reason: 'Machine-like consistency',
         variance 
       });
       this.resetStats();
    } else if (variance < 50 && meanLatency > 40 && meanLatency < 80) {
       this.addEvent('rhythm_anomaly', {
         severity: 'warning',
         reason: 'Likely transcription pattern',
         variance
       });
       this.resetStats();
    }
  }

  private resetStats() {
    this.count = 0;
    this.mean = 0;
    this.M2 = 0;
  }

  public trackFocus(focused: boolean) {
    if (!focused) this.addEvent('focus_loss', { timestamp: Date.now() });
  }

  private addEvent(type: IntegrityEventType, data: any) {
    const event = { type, timestamp: Date.now(), data };
    this.buffer.push(event);
    if (['velocity_spike', 'rhythm_anomaly', 'linearity_alert'].includes(type)) {
      this.onAnomaly(event);
    }
  }

  public flush() {
    const events = [...this.buffer];
    this.buffer =;
    return events;
  }
}
2. The Sensors: hooks/useKeystrokeDynamics.ts

This hook now listens for the cursor movement to feed the Linearity Index.

TypeScript
// hooks/useKeystrokeDynamics.ts
import { useEffect, useRef } from 'react';
import { IntegrityTracker } from '@/lib/IntegrityTracker';
import { useAbly } from '@/hooks/useAbly';

export function useKeystrokeDynamics(sessionId: string, editorInstance: any) {
  const tracker = useRef<IntegrityTracker | null>(null);
  const { channel } = useAbly(sessionId, 'tracker');

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!sessionId ||!editorInstance) return;

    tracker.current = new IntegrityTracker(sessionId, (event) => {
      console.warn('Integrity Alert:', event);
      channel?.publish('integrity_alert', event);
    });

    // 1. Keystrokes
    const keyDisposable = editorInstance.onKeyDown((e: any) => {
      tracker.current?.trackKeystroke(e.code);
    });

    // 2. Cursor Movement (CRITICAL for Linearity Index)
    const cursorDisposable = editorInstance.onDidChangeCursorPosition((e: any) => {
      tracker.current?.trackCursor(e.position.lineNumber, e.position.column);
    });

    // 3. Content Changes (For Bulk Insert)
    const contentDisposable = editorInstance.onDidChangeModelContent((e: any) => {
      const changes = e.changes;
      if (changes) {
        tracker.current?.trackContentChange(changes.text.length, changes.text);
      }
    });

    // 4. Paste
    const pasteDisposable = editorInstance.onDidPaste((e: any) => {
      tracker.current?.addEvent('paste', { range: e.range });
    });

    // 5. Focus
    const handleBlur = () => tracker.current?.trackFocus(false);
    window.addEventListener('blur', handleBlur);

    // 6. Flush to DB
    const flushInterval = setInterval(async () => {
      const events = tracker.current?.flush();
      if (events && events.length > 0) {
        await fetch('/api/events', {
          method: 'POST',
          body: JSON.stringify({ sessionId, events }),
        });
      }
    }, 5000);

    return () => {
      keyDisposable.dispose();
      cursorDisposable.dispose();
      contentDisposable.dispose();
      pasteDisposable.dispose();
      window.removeEventListener('blur', handleBlur);
      clearInterval(flushInterval);
    };
  }, [sessionId, editorInstance, channel]);
}

// Add this scoring map to your events handler
const SCORE_IMPACT = {
  'paste': -2,
  'focus_loss': -1,
  'velocity_spike': -5,        // Physical impossibility (bot)
  'rhythm_anomaly': -10,       // Statistical improbability (bot/script)
  'linearity_alert': -15,      // Behavioral improbability (transcription)
  'bulk_insert': -20           // Injection attack
};

//... inside the handler loop...
let scoreDrop = 0;
events.forEach(e => {
  scoreDrop += SCORE_IMPACT[e.type] |

| 0;
});

// Update session integrity score in MongoDB
await db.collection('sessions').updateOne(
  { sessionId },
  { $inc: { integrityScore: scoreDrop } }
);

Bot Test: Use a macro to type text at exactly 50ms intervals. Result: Rhythm Anomaly (Critical).

  ---
  Problem: FizzBuzz

  Write a function that prints numbers 1 to n, but:
  - For multiples of 3, print "Fizz"

  - For multiples of both, print "FizzBuzz"

  def fizzbuzz(n):

          elif i % 3 == 0:
              print("Fizz")
          elif i % 5 == 0:
              print("Buzz")

  # Two Sum: Find indices of two numbers that add to target
  def two_sum(nums, target):
      seen = {}
      for i, num in enumerate(nums):
          diff = target - num
          if diff in seen:
              return [seen[diff], i]
          seen[num] = i
      return []

  print(two_sum([2, 7, 11, 15], 9))  # [0, 1]

  Fix 1: Added debugging for sync issue

  Open browser DevTools console on BOTH candidate and reviewer tabs. You should see:
  - Candidate: [useAbly] publishCode called and Published code_update successfully
  - Reviewer: [useAbly] Subscribing to code_update and Received code_update message

  Fix 2: LinearityIndex now uses COMPOUND detection

  Before: Flag if >95% linearity (too aggressive - catches fast typists)

  Now: Flag ONLY if BOTH:
  95% linearity AND
  2. Suspicious rhythm (variance < 100 OR low variance + transcription speed)
  ┌────────────────────────────┬───────────┬────────────────────────────────┬──────────┐
  │          Scenario          │ Linearity │             Rhythm             │  Result  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ Skilled typist typing fast │ High      │ Normal variance                │ NO ALERT │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ AI transcription (Cluely)  │ High      │ Low variance, consistent speed │ ALERT    │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ Normal coding with edits   │ Low       │ Any                            │ NO ALERT │
  └────────────────────────────┴───────────┴────────────────────────────────┴──────────┘

❯ So it's connecting now, but what was the issue before? Technically speaking, the reviewer dashboard was not open initially. It was after I progressed significantly with the code that I    
  then opened it. Could that have been where the issue came from?                                                                                                                             

⏺ Yes, that's exactly the issue!

  Ably is real-time pub/sub - it only delivers messages to subscribers who are connected at the time of publishing. There's no message history by default.

  What happened:
  1. Candidate types → publishes code_update messages
  2. Reviewer opens LATER → subscribes to channel
  3. Reviewer missed all previous messages → sees empty/initial code

  Solutions:
  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Approach        │ Complexity │                     Description                     │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1. Store code in DB   │ Easy       │ Save latest code to MongoDB, load on reviewer open  │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2. Ably History       │ Medium     │ Use Ably's rewind feature to replay last N messages │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3. State sync request │ Medium     │ Reviewer requests current state, candidate responds │
  └───────────────────────┴────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Recommended: Option 1 - Store code snapshots in MongoDB. When reviewer opens, load the latest code from DB instead of starting empty.

  Want me to implement this? It would:
  1. Save code to MongoDB every few seconds (or on significant changes)
  2. Load latest code when reviewer dashboard opens
  3. Then continue with real-time sync for new changes

❯ yes implement it

⏺ This is a really important insight. Let me think through this deeply.

  The Core Problem

  You're describing a legitimate use case that looks similar to AI cheating:
  - Legitimate: Looking at reference code and typing it (learning, following tutorials)
  - Cheating: Looking at Cluely overlay and typing AI-generated answers

  Both involve "transcription" - but there's a KEY difference I just realized:

  Critical Insight: Focus Loss is Actually GOOD

  Cluely is an OVERLAY - the user never switches tabs. They see AI answers on the same screen.

  Legitimate copying requires TAB SWITCHING - you look at docs, switch back, type, switch again.

  So our current system is BACKWARDS:
  - We're penalizing focus_loss at -1 each
  - But focus_loss is actually evidence of LEGITIMATE behavior!
  - Cluely users have NO focus_loss because the overlay is always visible!

  The Fix

  1. Remove focus_loss penalty - it's penalizing legitimate behavior
  2. Add inverse detection - sustained typing WITHOUT focus_loss is MORE suspicious
  3. Be more conservative overall

⏺ You just discovered a fundamental limitation of this approach.

  The Hard Truth

  From the computer's perspective, these look identical:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │               Scenario               │       What computer sees        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Looking at phone and typing          │ Sustained typing, no focus_loss │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Looking at Cluely overlay and typing │ Sustained typing, no focus_loss │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Looking at second monitor with docs  │ Sustained typing, no focus_loss │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Looking at printed notes             │ Sustained typing, no focus_loss │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘
  We cannot distinguish between them.

  What This Means

  1. This problem is genuinely hard - there's no magic solution
  2. Detection is probabilistic, not definitive - we raise suspicion, not prove guilt
  3. Multiple signals matter - no single metric is conclusive
  4. Human judgment required - the recruiter makes the final call

  What We CAN Detect Reliably
  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Signal              │ Confidence │                Why                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ velocity_spike (<30ms)          │ High       │ Physically impossible for humans  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ paste (>50 chars)               │ High       │ Direct copy-paste detected        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bulk_insert                     │ High       │ Code appearing without keystrokes │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ rhythm_anomaly (variance <30)   │ Medium     │ Robotic consistency               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ linearity_alert + no focus_loss │ Low        │ Could be phone/paper/overlay      │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘
  Where We Are

  The system is working as designed - it's providing integrity signals, not verdicts. Your phone scenario is a legitimate edge case that proves:

  1. A score of 100 doesn't mean "definitely not cheating"
  2. A score below 100 doesn't mean "definitely cheating"
  3. The recruiter needs to interpret the signals in context

  Honest Assessment

  For a live proctored interview (with video), this system adds value because:
  - The interviewer can see if the candidate is looking at their phone
  - Combined with behavioral data, patterns become clearer

  For a take-home/unproctored test, cheating detection is much harder. The system can only flag statistical anomalies.

  Do you want to continue building this, knowing these limitations? Or pivot the approach?

What are your thoughts on all this gemini?
The "phone cheating" scenario you described—where a candidate manually types code from a separate device without interacting with the computer's OS—is indeed the "final boss" of remote proctoring. It exposes the fundamental limit of software-only solutions: Software cannot see the physical world.

However, you do not need to pivot. You just need to reframe your value proposition. You are not selling "impossible-to-cheat" software (which doesn't exist); you are selling "Defense in Depth."

1. The "Swiss Cheese" Security Model

In cybersecurity, no single layer is perfect. You layer imperfect defenses so that holes don't align.

Layer 1 (Velocity): Catches copy-pasters and script injections.

Layer 2 (Linearity): Catches AI transcribers (Cluely/overlays).

Layer 3 (Focus): Catches tab-switchers.

Layer 4 (The Missing Piece): Behavioral & Environmental constraints.

Your tool provides Layers 1, 2, and 3. Layer 4 is where you integrate Human Process into your software.

2. How to "Catch" the Phone User (Without invasive AI)

Since you cannot detect the phone software-wise, you must detect the human behavior required to use the phone.

A. The "Burst & Pause" Metric (Cognitive Load)

When a human thinks and codes, they type in small bursts (concepts), pause to think, then type again. When a human reads from a phone and types, they enter a "Data Entry" mode:

High Frequency of "Micro-Pauses": They have to look up, read 3-5 words, look down/at screen, type 3-5 words, look up again.

Metric: Gaze Latency Cycle. If the typing rhythm matches a "Read-Type-Read-Type" oscillation (e.g., 2 seconds typing, 1 second pause, repeat) consistently, it flags as "Transcription from External Source."

B. The "Solution Dump" Anomaly

A candidate using a phone often waits until the entire solution is generated on their phone before they start typing.

Normal: Code grows incrementally over 30 minutes.

Cheater: 10 minutes of silence (generating/reading), followed by 20 minutes of furious, linear typing.

Detection: Flag sessions where Code Velocity is zero for the first 30% of the interview and then spikes to 100% without edits.

3. Recommendation: The "Probabilistic" Pivot

Don't sell "Cheat Proof." Sell "Cheating Probabilities."

Change your UI from "CHEAT DETECTED" (Binary) to a "Risk Signals Dashboard."

The Recruiter Dashboard should look like this:

🟢 Integrity: High

⚠️ Anomalies Detected:

High Linearity (98%) - Suspected transcription.

Rhythmic Pauses - Suspected reading from external source.

Zero Focus Loss - Candidate never checked documentation (Suspicious for a complex problem).

Your Pitch:

"We don't claim to catch 100% of cheaters. We claim to flag the statistical anomalies that indicate a candidate isn't thinking like an engineer. If they type a complex algorithm perfectly linearly without checking docs and without pausing to think, you should ask them to explain that code line-by-line."

4. Immediate Action Plan (Day 3 & 4)

Keep the current stack: It's working.

Refine the Logic: Implement the "Focus Loss = GOOD" change we discussed.

Add the "Oscillation" Check:

In IntegrityTracker.ts, track the variance of pause durations.

If user pauses for ~1.5s every ~15 keystrokes consistently, flag as external_read_pattern.

Accept the Limit: Acknowledging this limitation makes you look more credible to investors/CTOs. They know software can't see phones. Admitting it and offering a heuristic solution (rhythm analysis) shows you understand the domain deeply.

Jan 15, 3:07 PM

Temporal Signatures of Cognition: A Comprehensive Analysis of Keystroke Dynamics in Differentiating Composition, Transcription, and Artificial Text Production1. Introduction: The Behavioral Biometric LandscapeThe digital age has fundamentally transformed the act of writing, shifting the primary mode of text production from the inscription of ink on paper to the depression of keys on a peripheral device. This shift has inadvertently created a new window into the human mind: keystroke dynamics. Unlike physiological biometrics—such as fingerprints, iris scans, or facial geometry, which rely on static anatomical features—keystroke dynamics is a behavioral biometric. It captures the temporal cadence, rhythmic consistencies, and hesitation patterns inherent in the neuromuscular execution of typing. This field, situated at the intersection of cognitive psychology, motor control theory, cybersecurity, and forensic linguistics, posits that the timing of neural impulses to the fingers is as unique to an individual as their signature or gait.1The historical roots of this discipline extend back to the era of telegraphy. During the Second World War, military intelligence officers utilized a technique known as "The Fist of the Sender" to identify specific telegraph operators. By analyzing the unique rhythm of dots and dashes—the "swing" of the operator—allies could distinguish between friend and foe, or identify when a known operator had been replaced, even if the message content remained standard.2 Today, this principle has been digitized and refined with millisecond-precision timing, serving applications ranging from continuous authentication in banking systems to the detection of academic dishonesty in higher education.The central thesis of this report is that the cognitive processes underlying "organic composition" (the creation of original thought) and "transcription" (the copying of existing text) produce fundamentally distinct neuro-motor signatures. These signatures are quantifiable through granular metrics such as flight time, dwell time, inter-keystroke intervals (IKI), and burst length.3 Furthermore, as generative artificial intelligence (AI) tools like Large Language Models (LLMs) become ubiquitous, the forensic challenge has evolved. Analysts must now distinguish not only between different human users but between a human struggling to formulate a complex thought and a human merely transcribing a machine-generated output. This distinction relies on the hypothesis that the cognitive load of "planning" and "translation" creates specific dysfluencies that are absent in the linear, memory-buffered process of transcription.52. Theoretical Frameworks of Text ProductionTo interpret the raw data of keystroke logs, one must first ground the analysis in established cognitive models of writing. The data—timestamps of key presses and releases—are meaningless without a theoretical understanding of why a user pauses or speeds up.2.1 The Cognitive Architecture: Hayes and Flower ModelThe seminal model proposed by Hayes and Flower (1980) and subsequently revised (Hayes, 2012) decomposes the writing process into three distinct, recursive components: planning, translation, and transcription.3Planning: This is the highest-level cognitive function, involving goal setting, idea generation, and organization. It places significant demands on the central executive component of working memory. The writer must retrieve information from long-term memory, evaluate its relevance to the topic, and structure it logically.8Translation: This phase involves converting abstract ideas into specific linguistic structures—selecting vocabulary (lexical retrieval) and formulating syntactic frames (sentences and clauses).3Transcription: In the cognitive sense, transcription refers to the motor execution of these linguistic structures. It involves the retrieval of orthographic forms (spelling) and the generation of motor programs to guide the fingers to the correct keys.8Crucially, these processes compete for limited cognitive resources within the writer's working memory. This competition is often described as the "transcription bottleneck." When transcription skills—typing or handwriting—are not fully automated, they consume attentional resources that could otherwise be dedicated to higher-level composing tasks like planning and revising.82.2 The Transcription Bottleneck and Working MemoryThe "Simple View of Writing" suggests that writing complexity is the product of transcription skills and executive functions. For novice writers, or those with specific learning disabilities (SLD) such as dysgraphia, the sheer effort of recalling the spelling of a word or locating a key on the board can derail the train of thought.8Research indicates that if students do not develop fluency with transcription skills by approximately Grade 3, this lack of automaticity becomes a constraint on their writing development in later grades.8 The cognitive load of non-automatic transcription forces the writer to minimize the composing process. Instead of engaging in "knowledge transforming" (developing and refining thoughts), the writer reverts to "knowledge telling"—a simpler, forward-moving idea-generation activity where thoughts are written down as they occur, with minimal planning or revision.8This bottleneck has a direct observable effect on keystroke dynamics. Writers with poor transcription automaticity exhibit:Shorter Language Bursts: They produce fewer words in a single run before stopping.Longer Pauses: Significant time is spent on motor planning and orthographic retrieval.Reduced Text Quality: Because working memory is monopolized by the mechanics of typing, less capacity remains for monitoring coherence and structure.3In contrast, expert writers have automated the transcription process. For them, typing is "transparent"—a direct conduit from thought to screen. This automaticity allows for parallel processing: an expert can plan the next sentence while mechanically typing the current one. However, even experts face bottlenecks during periods of high cognitive load, such as resolving a complex argument, which manifest as distinct "planning pauses" at grammatical boundaries.112.3 Transcription as a Copying TaskIt is vital to distinguish the cognitive definition of "transcription" (the motor act of writing) from the forensic definition of a "transcription task" (copying text). When a user copies text—whether from a book, a website, or a generative AI window—the cognitive architecture changes radically. The "planning" and "translation" phases are largely bypassed. The writer is no longer generating ideas or formulating sentences; they are merely buffering a string of characters in their working memory (specifically, the phonological loop and visuo-spatial sketchpad) and executing the motor commands to type them.5This shift results in a fundamental alteration of the keystroke rhythm. Authentic composition is characterized by hesitancy, recursion, and non-linearity. The writer types, pauses to think, backspaces to revise, and types again. In contrast, a transcription task is characterized by linearity and rhythmic regularity, punctuated only by "read-type" pauses where the user acquires the next chunk of text from the source.5 This distinction is the cornerstone of detecting AI-assisted plagiarism where the user manually types out AI-generated content.3. The Biomechanics and Chronometry of KeystrokesTo operationalize these cognitive theories into detection systems, we must examine the specific metrics available from standard input devices. The analysis of keystroke dynamics relies on quantifying the interaction between the user's physiology and the mechanical properties of the keyboard.3.1 Fundamental Metrics: Dwell and FlightThe raw data stream from a keyboard consists of "Key Down" and "Key Up" events, each associated with a timestamp. From these events, two primary atomic features are derived:Dwell Time (Hold Time): This is the duration for which a specific key is depressed (Key Down to Key Up). While partially influenced by the mechanical actuation point of the switch (e.g., membrane vs. mechanical keys), dwell time is also a behavioral characteristic. Some users are "heavy" typists who linger on keys, while others use a staccato "peck" style. Dwell time has been shown to be consistent for an individual regardless of their overall typing speed, making it a robust biometric feature.1Flight Time (Latency / Inter-Keystroke Interval - IKI): This is the interval between releasing one key and pressing the next (Key Up to Next Key Down), or sometimes measured as Press-to-Press latency. This metric is the most information-rich as it captures the cognitive processing speed between characters.2Table 1 illustrates the timing features available from a sequence of two keystrokes ($K_1$ and $K_2$).FeatureDefinitionCognitive/Motor SignificanceHold Time ($H_1$)$Time(K_1^{Up}) - Time(K_1^{Down})$Motor decisiveness, finger strength, keyboard mechanics.Flight Time ($F_{1,2}$)$Time(K_2^{Down}) - Time(K_1^{Up})$Cognitive processing, finger travel distance, hesitation.Press-to-Press ($P_{1,2}$)$Time(K_2^{Down}) - Time(K_1^{Down})$Overall typing speed (inverse of WPM).Release-to-Release ($R_{1,2}$)$Time(K_2^{Up}) - Time(K_1^{Up})$Rhythmic consistency.3.2 Digraph and Trigraph LatenciesBeyond single keys, the transitions between specific pairs (digraphs) and triplets (trigraphs) of keys reveal the "internal wiring" of a user's motor cortex. The time required to type "th" is typically much shorter than the time to type "xq" because "th" is a high-frequency digraph in English that proficient typists execute as a single "chunked" motor program.1This phenomenon creates a "latency map" unique to the user. For example, a right-handed user might be statistically faster at reaching keys on the right side of the board. Furthermore, specific sequences reveal "roll-over" habits—where a second key is pressed before the first is released. This "negative latency" or key overlap is a hallmark of high-speed, fluent typing.14 Conversely, the "Word Initiation Effect" describes the phenomenon where the latency before the first character of a word is significantly longer than the latencies between characters within the word, reflecting the cognitive cost of retrieving the word from the mental lexicon.133.3 Hardware Limitations and Quantization NoiseA critical, often overlooked factor in keystroke analysis is the precision of the recording hardware. Most standard USB keyboards operate with a polling rate of roughly 125 Hz, meaning the state of the keyboard is checked every 8 milliseconds. This introduces "quantization noise" into the data. An IKI that appears to be 80ms might actually be 76ms or 84ms, snapped to the nearest polling cycle.15Research using high-precision timers (e.g., Black Box ToolKit) alongside web-based collectors (like jsPsych) indicates that while web browsers introduce some variability, the primary source of "graininess" is the USB polling rate. In histograms of inter-keystroke intervals, this manifests as "comb-like" distributions where values cluster around multiples of 8ms (e.g., 80, 88, 96ms).15 For forensic analysis, this means that extremely subtle differences in timing (e.g., 2-3ms) may be artifacts of the hardware rather than genuine behavioral differences, necessitating the use of statistical smoothing or binning techniques in the analysis algorithms.4. The Phenomenology of the PauseIf keystrokes are the "sound" of writing, pauses are the silence that provides the structure. In the flow of text production, pauses are not merely the absence of activity; they are the locus of cognitive processing. They occur when the writer's working memory is engaged in planning the next phrase, retrieving a difficult spelling, or revising a previous thought.74.1 The Threshold Debate: Defining "Graphic Inactivity"A significant methodological challenge in the field is defining what constitutes a meaningful pause. The literature reveals a lack of consensus, with thresholds varying based on the granularity of the research question.The 2,000ms (2s) Threshold: Historically, a 2-second threshold was the standard for isolating "higher-level" writing processes. Pauses exceeding 2 seconds were assumed to be devoted to macro-planning, rhetorical decision-making, or global revision.7 This threshold is useful for analyzing the broad strokes of composition but is too coarse to detect the micro-hesitations associated with transcription difficulty.The 200ms - 400ms Threshold: For analyzing "transcription fluency"—the automaticity of finding keys—shorter thresholds are required. Research on handwriting suggests average execution pauses are around 400ms.7 In skilled typing, IKIs often fall below 200ms. Pauses in the 200ms–500ms range typically reflect inter-letter transitions or micro-planning at the syllable level.16Adaptive Thresholds: Recent studies argue against fixed thresholds. A 500ms pause might be a significant cognitive delay for a typist capable of 100 WPM, but merely the standard "seek time" for a typist at 30 WPM. Therefore, pauses are increasingly defined relative to the individual's baseline typing speed (e.g., any IKI greater than 3 standard deviations above the mean).74.2 Pause Location and Cognitive LoadThe location of a pause is as diagnostic as its duration. In fluent, organic composition, pauses are not randomly distributed; they cluster at grammatical boundaries.Clause and Sentence Boundaries: Long pauses (>1s) are overwhelmingly found at the beginnings of sentences and clauses. This supports the "burst" hypothesis, where a writer plans a syntactic unit, executes it, and then pauses to plan the next.11Word Boundaries: Shorter pauses occur between words, reflecting the retrieval of the next lexical item.Within-Word Pauses: Pauses within a word are rare in fluent writing and usually indicate a spelling difficulty (orthographic retrieval issue) or a motor execution error.7In contrast, pauses in transcription (copying) tasks follow a different logic. They are dictated by the "visual span" or buffer capacity of the writer. A copyist might pause in the middle of a sentence—or even a phrase—simply because they have reached the end of the text chunk they memorized from the source document. These "read-type" pauses occur at arbitrary syntactic locations, distinct from the "planning-type" pauses of composition.54.3 Pauses in Speech vs. WritingComparing writing pauses to speech pauses highlights the universality of language production constraints. In speech, "natural" pauses typically last around 0.6 seconds within sentences and up to 1.0 second between sentences, maintaining a roughly 1:2 ratio.18Read Speech vs. Spontaneous Speech: In "read speech" (reading aloud), pauses occur strictly at syntactic boundaries (commas, periods). In "spontaneous speech," pauses are more erratic and can occur mid-phrase as the speaker searches for a word (filled pauses like "um" or "uh").19Parallel to Typing: This parallels the distinction in keystroke dynamics. "Transcription typing" is the manual equivalent of "read speech"—mechanically regulated by the source text. "Composition typing" is the equivalent of "spontaneous speech"—regulated by the internal generation of ideas. Just as listeners can distinguish reading from speaking by rhythm alone, algorithms can distinguish copying from writing by the pause distribution.115. Burst Dynamics and Working MemoryIf pauses represent the loading of the cognitive buffer, "bursts" represent the unloading. A "writing burst" or "language burst" is defined as a sequence of text produced during a period of active writing, terminated by a pause of a specified duration (e.g., >2 seconds) or an editing event.35.1 The Anatomy of a Burst: P-Bursts and R-BurstsBursts are the fundamental units of text production. Research distinguishes between "P-bursts" (Production/Planning bursts) where new text is generated, and "R-bursts" (Revision bursts) where text is deleted or modified.The length of a burst—measured in words or characters—is one of the most robust indicators of writing proficiency. Expert writers produce longer bursts than novices.3 This is because experts have automated the lower-level transcription skills, allowing them to hold larger chunks of linguistic content in their working memory and "dump" them onto the screen in a sustained flow.Conversely, novice writers or those with learning disabilities often produce "fragmented" bursts. They may write only one or two words before pausing. This fragmentation is often due to the "transcription bottleneck": the effort of typing is so high that the working memory buffer decays before the full sentence can be transcribed, forcing the writer to stop and reconstruct the thought.35.2 Burst Variance: The Signature of AuthenticityThe distribution of burst lengths is a critical forensic marker.Authentic Composition: Exhibits high variance in burst length. A writer might produce a long, fluent sentence (a long burst of 15 words), followed by a series of short, staccato bursts (1-2 words) as they revise a specific adjective or correct a typo. This mixture reflects the recursive nature of writing: generate, evaluate, revise.5Transcription (Copying): Bursts in copying tasks tend to be more uniform. The length of the burst is constrained by the writer's "copying span"—the amount of text they can retain in short-term memory while looking from the source to the screen (typically 5-10 words). This produces a rhythmic, almost mechanical burst pattern. Copyists rarely engage in the "micro-revision" bursts that characterize authentic drafting, as they are not evaluating the content, only its fidelity to the source.55.3 Working Memory ConstraintsWorking memory capacity is the engine that drives burst length. In composition, the "Central Executive" and "Episodic Buffer" are heavily engaged in linking long-term memory (ideas) with the "Phonological Loop" (language formulation).In transcription tasks, the cognitive load shifts. The "Central Executive" is less involved in generation and more involved in "switching" attention between the source and the output. The burst length in copying is a direct measure of the capacity of the Phonological Loop and the Visuo-Spatial Sketchpad. This explains why transcription fluency (typing speed) is a strong predictor of burst length: the faster one types, the more information can be offloaded from the fading working memory buffer.36. Developmental and Clinical PerspectivesKeystroke dynamics is not only a forensic tool but a diagnostic one. The patterns of pauses and bursts evolve with age and are significantly altered by cognitive and motor disorders.6.1 Developmental TrajectoryChildren in mid-primary grades (e.g., 3rd vs. 5th graders) show marked differences in keystroke behavior.Grade 3: Writers are still developing basic transcription skills. Their writing is characterized by frequent, long pauses (often >2s) even within words, as they struggle with letter formation and spelling. Graph inactivity can account for up to 50% of the total composition time.7Grade 5 and Beyond: As transcription becomes automated, the number and duration of pauses decrease. The pauses that remain begin to cluster at clause boundaries, signaling the shift from "thinking about spelling" to "thinking about content".76.2 Dyslexia and DysgraphiaStudents with Specific Learning Disabilities (SLD) such as dyslexia (impairment in reading/spelling) and dysgraphia (impairment in handwriting/motor coding) exhibit distinct keystroke profiles.Pause Patterns: Regardless of the mode (handwriting or keyboarding), students with dyslexia show more frequent and longer pauses than age-matched peers. These pauses often occur within words, reflecting orthographic uncertainty.9Burst Length: Due to the high cognitive cost of transcription, these students produce significantly shorter language bursts. The "transcription bottleneck" is severe; the cognitive resources needed to hold a sentence in memory are drained by the effort of spelling it.9Mode Comparison: Interestingly, while keyboarding can sometimes alleviate the motor demands of handwriting for dysgraphic students, the dyslexia group often shows slower composing rates and more spelling errors on keyboards compared to typical peers. However, the total number of words produced is often higher on a keyboard than by hand, suggesting that for some, the keyboard unblocks the bottleneck slightly, even if it remains narrower than for neurotypical students.96.3 Mild Cognitive Impairment (MCI)Emerging research suggests keystroke dynamics can serve as a "digital biomarker" for early-stage cognitive decline.Post-Pause Latency: Individuals with Mild Cognitive Impairment (MCI) show longer latencies immediately following a pause. This suggests a difficulty in "re-engaging" the motor or cognitive plan after a break.21Lexical Simplification: After a pause, individuals with MCI are more likely to use high-frequency, simpler words, whereas healthy controls may use the pause to retrieve a more complex or precise term. This "post-pause lexical retrieval" signature is highly predictive, with some models achieving ~79% accuracy in screening for MCI based on speech and typing patterns.217. Forensic Differentiation: Composition vs. Transcription vs. AIThe primary application of this research in the current landscape is the differentiation of three modes of text production: Organic Composition, Manual Transcription (Plagiarism), and AI-Assisted Generation.7.1 Signatures of Organic CompositionOrganic composition is a non-linear, recursive process. Its behavioral fingerprint includes:High Revision Rate: Authentic writers constantly monitor their output. Keystroke logs show a high density of Backspace, Delete, and Arrow key usage. Revisions are not just error corrections but "content revisions"—changing a word for tone, restructuring a sentence, or deleting a paragraph.5Syntactic Pausing: Pauses align with grammatical structures (clauses, sentences) as the writer plans the next unit of meaning.11Burst Variability: A natural oscillation between long "flow" bursts and short "editing" bursts.5Latency Variations: Higher latencies are observed before complex words or low-frequency vocabulary, reflecting lexical retrieval time. This is known as the "word frequency effect" in typing.117.2 Signatures of Manual Transcription (Copying)When a user acts as a human bridge between a source (book/AI) and the document, the signature changes:Linearity: The text is produced in a strictly forward-moving fashion. There are very few insertions of text into previously written sentences. The cursor rarely moves backwards significantly.5"Read-Type" Pauses: Pauses occur at regular intervals determined by the user's working memory buffer, often breaking syntactic boundaries. A user might pause in the middle of a sentence simply because they forgot the rest of the sequence they were copying.5Rhythmic Regularity: Once a chunk is memorized, it is typed with high rhythmic consistency. The "cognitive" pauses between words are minimized because the words are already selected; only the motor execution remains. This results in a lower Coefficient of Variation (CV) for IKIs within the burst.22Lack of Content Revision: Copyists rarely edit the text they are transcribing unless they make a typo. They do not engage in rhetorical revision.57.3 Signatures of AI-Assisted WritingAI plagiarism manifests in two primary behaviors:Copy-Pasting: The user generates text in a chatbot and pastes it. This is detected by "impossible" speeds—an entire paragraph appearing in a single timestamp event, or a typing speed exceeding human physiological limits (>300 WPM sustained).5Manual Transcription of AI Output: To bypass copy-paste detectors, users may generate text on a secondary screen and manually type it. Forensic models can detect this with high accuracy (up to 99% in some studies) by identifying the "transcription signature" (regular pauses, low revision, linearity) within the keystroke log. The model effectively recognizes that the user is "reading" onto the screen rather than "writing".57.4 The "Looking Away" PhenomenonA specific behavioral marker of transcription is the "focus shift." In organic composition, the writer's visual attention is primarily on the text being produced (to monitor for errors) or the keyboard. In transcription, the user must repeatedly shift attention to the source text.Although standard keystroke logging cannot track eye movements, the consequences of looking away are visible in the timing data. "Looking away" creates a distinct pause profile—a regular, long pause followed by a rapid burst of typing. If the user is a touch typist but transcribing, the rhythm is smoother but still segmented. If they must look at the keys and the source, the pattern becomes highly fragmented.24Table 2 summarizes the differentiating metrics between these modes.MetricOrganic CompositionTranscription (Copying/AI)Burst Length DistributionHigh Variance (Long P-bursts, Short R-bursts)Uniform (limited by visual buffer)Pause LocationGrammatical/Syntactic boundariesArbitrary/Visual boundariesRevision RateHigh (deletions, insertions, navigation)Low (mostly typo corrections)IKI Variance (CV)High (bursty)Lower (rhythmic within bursts)Typing SpeedVariable (slows during planning)Generally faster (motor-limited only)Edit DistanceHigh (final text differs from initial drafts)Low (linear production)Focus ShiftsMinimal (internal focus)Frequent (external focus)8. Algorithmic Approaches and Machine LearningThe complexity of keystroke data necessitates advanced computational methods for analysis. Simple statistical thresholds are often insufficient to capture the nuanced interaction of features.8.1 Feature EngineeringEffective machine learning models rely on a rich set of engineered features:Global Features: Total session time, average WPM, error rate (Backspace count / Total keystrokes), total number of pauses > 2s.9Local Features: Latencies for specific key n-grams (digraphs, trigraphs). The sequence "th" vs "he" provides highly discriminatory power.2Sequence Features: The entire time-series of IKIs can be treated as a signal. Deep learning models use Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) to analyze the "shape" of this signal, capturing long-range dependencies and rhythmic patterns.258.2 Machine Learning ArchitecturesRandom Forests: Classical ML algorithms like Random Forests have proven highly effective (99% accuracy) in binary classification tasks (Authentic vs. Transcribed) by leveraging aggregated features like pause counts and revision statistics.5TypeNet and Deep Metric Learning: For more complex tasks like "cheating ring" detection, researchers employ deep metric learning. Models like TypeNet use Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks to create a 128-dimensional embedding vector for a typing session. By calculating the Euclidean distance between these vectors, the system can determine if two different accounts were accessed by the same physical typist (a "contract cheater").6Content-Agnostic Models: Crucially, these models can function without analyzing the content of the text, preserving user privacy. They look only at the behavior (timing), making them language-independent and resilient to paraphrasing.258.3 Performance MetricsThe reliability of these systems is evaluated using standard biometric metrics:False Accept Rate (FAR): The probability that an impostor (or transcriber) is accepted as the genuine user (or composer).False Reject Rate (FRR): The probability that a genuine user is incorrectly flagged.Equal Error Rate (EER): The point where FAR and FRR are equal. Lower EER indicates a more robust system.F1 Score: In plagiarism detection, F1 scores above 97% have been achieved for structured settings, and ~87% for detecting paraphrasing.69. Professional Transcription: Human Performance BenchmarksTo contextualize the data on "transcription typing," it is useful to look at the professional domain of audio transcription, where the goal is maximum speed and accuracy.9.1 Turnaround Time (TAT) and RatiosThe industry standard for professional transcription is a 4:1 ratio—it takes approximately four hours of work to transcribe one hour of clear audio.27 This ratio accounts for the need to pause, rewind, and research terminology.Typing Speed vs. Task Speed: While a professional might type at 80-100 WPM, their effective "transcription speed" is much lower due to the cognitive load of listening and processing. This highlights that even for experts, transcription is not a purely motor task; it involves significant cognitive processing (decoding speech, verifying context).289.2 Factors Influencing SpeedSeveral factors degrade transcription fluency, creating "noise" in the keystroke signal:Audio Quality: Background noise forces frequent pauses and rewinds.Speaker Count: Identifying multiple speakers requires cognitive "context switching," leading to longer pauses between turns.Content Complexity: Technical or medical terminology requires "research pauses"—stopping to look up a word. These pauses are distinct from "copying pauses" as they often involve switching windows or long periods of inactivity.27In a forensic context, if a student claims they were "just typing slowly" because they were thinking, but their profile matches a professional transcriber struggling with poor audio (frequent short rewinds/edits vs. long planning pauses), the alibi may not hold.10. Security, Authentication, and BiometricsBeyond plagiarism, keystroke dynamics is a powerful tool for identity verification (Authentication).10.1 Continuous AuthenticationTraditional authentication (passwords) is static and binary. Keystroke dynamics allows for Continuous Authentication. The system monitors the user's typing rhythm throughout the entire session. If an unauthorized user takes over a logged-in terminal, their typing rhythm will diverge from the authorized user's template, triggering a lockout. This is particularly valuable for high-security environments.210.2 Template Aging and RetrainingA major challenge in behavioral biometrics is "template aging." A user's typing style is not immutable. It changes with:Time: Proficiency increases or decreases with age.Health: Fatigue, injury, or intoxication.Equipment: Switching from a mechanical keyboard to a laptop membrane keyboard fundamentally alters dwell times and flight times.2To maintain low FRR, systems must employ "adaptive" mechanisms that update the user's template over time. Each successful, high-confidence login is used to slightly refine the stored profile, allowing the system to "learn" the user's evolving style.3010.3 Quality AssessmentNot all users are equally "unique." Some typists have very generic, rhythmic styles (high FAR risk), while others have highly idiosyncratic rhythms (low FAR risk). Recent research has proposed metrics to quantify the "quality" of a keystroke template. Users with "Bad" templates (high intra-user variance) may require additional authentication factors, while users with "Good" templates can rely more heavily on the biometric.3211. Privacy, Ethics, and Future DirectionsThe power of keystroke dynamics comes with significant ethical responsibilities.11.1 Soft Biometrics and PrivacyKeystroke data contains more than just identity; it contains "soft biometrics." Research has shown that keystroke patterns can reveal:Gender and Age: With reasonable accuracy.14Emotional State: Stress, anger, or neutrality.12Health Status: Early signs of neurodegenerative diseases like Parkinson's.33Collecting this data for security or anti-plagiarism purposes raises the risk of "function creep," where data collected for one purpose is used to infer sensitive personal information without consent. The "inference problem"—that behavioral data reveals internal states—is a major privacy hurdle.1411.2 Explainability in AIAs systems become more reliant on "Black Box" Deep Learning models (like TypeNet), the issue of explainability becomes critical. In an academic disciplinary hearing, it is insufficient to state "The AI says you cheated with 99% confidence." Administrators need explainable metrics—such as "This document contains zero revision events and a uniform pause structure consistent with copying"—to support the finding. The future of this field lies in "Explainable AI" (XAI) that can translate complex vector embeddings into human-understandable behavioral descriptions.612. ConclusionThe analysis of keystroke dynamics represents a paradigm shift in how we evaluate digital text. It moves the focus from the product—the static arrangement of words on a page—to the process—the temporal journey of their creation. By measuring the invisible chronometry of writing—the millisecond hesitations, the bursts of fluency, and the rhythms of revision—forensic analysts can now distinguish the cognitive struggle of authentic composition from the mechanical linearity of transcription.The evidence is robust and multifaceted. Authentic writing is a high-load cognitive task marked by variable bursts and planning pauses at syntactic boundaries. Transcription, whether from a book or an AI chatbot, is a linear, memory-limited task marked by rhythmic "reading" pauses and a conspicuous lack of revision. As generative AI continues to blur the line between human and machine creativity, the "process data" captured by keystroke logging may become the only reliable proof of human authorship. It verifies not just who wrote the text, but that the text was truly written—thought by thought, keystroke by keystroke—rather than merely processed. This technology, evolving from the telegraph operators of the 1940s to the deep neural networks of the 2020s, now stands as a critical guardian of integrity in the digital age.

Before you proceed with day 4, here's gemini's evaluation of our last session:                                                                   
  The "phone cheating" scenario you described—where a candidate manually types code from a separate device without interacting with the computer's 
  OS—is indeed the "final boss" of remote proctoring. It exposes the fundamental limit of software-only solutions: **Software cannot see the       
  physical world.**                                                                                                                                
                                                                                                                                                   
  However, you do not need to pivot. You just need to reframe your value proposition. You are not selling "impossible-to-cheat" software (which    
  doesn't exist); you are selling **"Defense in Depth."**                                                                                          
                                                                                                                                                   
  ### 1. The "Swiss Cheese" Security Model                                                                                                         
                                                                                                                                                   
  In cybersecurity, no single layer is perfect. You layer imperfect defenses so that holes don't align.                                            
                                                                                                                                                   
  * **Layer 1 (Velocity):** Catches copy-pasters and script injections.                                                                            
  * **Layer 2 (Linearity):** Catches AI transcribers (Cluely/overlays).                                                                            
  * **Layer 3 (Focus):** Catches tab-switchers.                                                                                                    
  * **Layer 4 (The Missing Piece):** **Behavioral & Environmental constraints.**                                                                   
                                                                                                                                                   
  Your tool provides Layers 1, 2, and 3. Layer 4 is where you integrate **Human Process** into your software.                                      
                                                                                                                                                   
  ### 2. How to "Catch" the Phone User (Without invasive AI)                                                                                       
                                                                                                                                                   
  Since you cannot detect the phone software-wise, you must detect the **human behavior** required to use the phone.                               
                                                                                                                                                   
  #### A. The "Burst & Pause" Metric (Cognitive Load)                                                                                              
                                                                                                                                                   
  When a human *thinks* and codes, they type in small bursts (concepts), pause to think, then type again.                                          
  When a human *reads* from a phone and types, they enter a **"Data Entry" mode**:                                                                 
                                                                                                                                                   
  * **High Frequency of "Micro-Pauses":** They have to look up, read 3-5 words, look down/at screen, type 3-5 words, look up again.                
  * **Metric:** `Gaze Latency Cycle`. If the typing rhythm matches a "Read-Type-Read-Type" oscillation (e.g., 2 seconds typing, 1 second pause,    
  repeat) consistently, it flags as "Transcription from External Source."                                                                          
                                                                                                                                                   
  #### B. The "Solution Dump" Anomaly                                                                                                              
                                                                                                                                                   
  A candidate using a phone often waits until the *entire* solution is generated on their phone before they start typing.                          
                                                                                                                                                   
  * **Normal:** Code grows incrementally over 30 minutes.                                                                                          
  * **Cheater:** 10 minutes of silence (generating/reading), followed by 20 minutes of furious, linear typing.                                     
  * **Detection:** Flag sessions where `Code Velocity` is zero for the first 30% of the interview and then spikes to 100% without edits.

⏺ Yes, it's highly relevant! It's the academic foundation for what Gemini suggested. Key takeaways:

  Directly applicable to TalonHunt:

  1. "Read-Type" Pauses (Section 7.2) - When copying, pauses occur at arbitrary syntactic locations (middle of sentences) based on visual buffer capacity, not at clause/sentence boundaries like authentic composition.
  2. Rhythmic Regularity - Transcribers have lower IKI variance within bursts because they're not thinking, just motor-executing.
  3. Burst Variance (Section 5.2) - Authentic writing has high variance in burst lengths (long flow bursts mixed with short revision bursts). Transcription has uniform burst lengths (~5-10 words per "copy chunk").
  4. Lack of Revision - Copyists rarely use Backspace/Delete except for typos. No "content revisions."

Day 4: The "Gaze Cycle" Detection Logic
You are absolutely right. To catch the "Phone Cheater" or the "Second Monitor Read-Only" user, we need to detect the mechanical rhythm of transcription.

When a human reads code from a phone to type it out, they are bound by their Working Memory Buffer.

Read: Look at phone (1.5s). Load ~10-15 characters into memory.

Type: Burst of 10-15 characters (2s).

Refill: Buffer empty. Stop. Look at phone (1.5s).

Repeat.

This creates a sinusoidal wave of activity (Oscillation) that is statistically distinct from thinking (which is irregular) or knowing (which is continuous).

Here is the code to detect this "Read-Type-Read-Type" heartbeat.

1. Update lib/IntegrityTracker.ts

We are adding a new detection engine: BurstAnalysis.

TypeScript
// lib/IntegrityTracker.ts

export type IntegrityEventType = 

| 'paste' 
| 'focus_loss' 
| 'velocity_spike' 
| 'rhythm_anomaly' 
| 'linearity_alert' 
| 'bulk_insert'
| 'read_pattern_warning'; // New event type

interface BurstData {
  length: number;      // How many chars typed in this burst
  pauseDuration: number; // How long was the pause before this burst?
  timestamp: number;
}

export class IntegrityTracker {
  //... existing state...
  
  // --- Oscillation Tracking State ---
  private currentBurstLength = 0;
  private burstHistory: BurstData =;
  private BURST_THRESHOLD = 1000; // 1s pause defines a "burst" boundary
  
  //... existing constructor...

  public trackKeystroke(char: string) {
    const now = Date.now();
    
    // Ignore start of session
    if (this.lastKeyTime === 0) {
      this.lastKeyTime = now;
      return;
    }

    const latency = now - this.lastKeyTime;
    
    // --- Burst & Oscillation Logic ---
    if (latency > this.BURST_THRESHOLD) {
      // Previous burst ended. Record it.
      if (this.currentBurstLength > 0) {
        this.recordBurst(this.currentBurstLength, latency);
      }
      this.currentBurstLength = 1; // Start new burst with this char
    } else {
      this.currentBurstLength++;
    }

    this.lastKeyTime = now;
    
    //... existing velocity/linearity checks...
  }

  private recordBurst(length: number, pauseDuration: number) {
    this.burstHistory.push({ length, pauseDuration, timestamp: Date.now() });
    
    // Keep last 20 bursts
    if (this.burstHistory.length > 20) {
      this.burstHistory.shift();
      this.analyzeOscillation();
    }
  }

  private analyzeOscillation() {
    // We need at least 10 bursts to detect a pattern
    if (this.burstHistory.length < 10) return;

    // 1. Calculate consistency of Burst Length (how much they memorize at once)
    const lengths = this.burstHistory.map(b => b.length);
    const meanLen = lengths.reduce((a, b) => a + b) / lengths.length;
    const varianceLen = lengths.reduce((a, b) => a + Math.pow(b - meanLen, 2), 0) / lengths.length;
    const stdDevLen = Math.sqrt(varianceLen);
    const cvLen = stdDevLen / meanLen; // Coefficient of Variation

    // 2. Calculate consistency of Pause Duration (how long it takes to read next chunk)
    const pauses = this.burstHistory.map(b => b.pauseDuration);
    const meanPause = pauses.reduce((a, b) => a + b) / pauses.length;
    const variancePause = pauses.reduce((a, b) => a + Math.pow(b - meanPause, 2), 0) / pauses.length;
    const stdDevPause = Math.sqrt(variancePause);
    const cvPause = stdDevPause / meanPause;

    // THE ALGORITHM:
    // Transcribers have LOW variance in both length (buffer size) and pause (reading speed).
    // Thinkers have HIGH variance (short edits vs long flow).
    
    // Thresholds: 
    // CV < 0.4 implies highly regular rhythm
    // Mean Burst Length between 5-20 chars (typical short-term memory buffer)
    // Mean Pause > 1s (time to look at phone/screen)
    
    if (cvLen < 0.4 && cvPause < 0.4 && meanLen > 5 && meanLen < 30 && meanPause > 1000) {
      this.addEvent('read_pattern_warning', {
        confidence: 'medium',
        pattern: 'Regular oscillation detected (Read-Type Cycle)',
        metrics: { meanBurstLength: meanLen, meanReadPause: meanPause, consistencyScore: (1 - cvLen) }
      });
      // Clear history to prevent spamming
      this.burstHistory =;
    }
  }

  //... existing methods...
}

So this solution we are building now, does it take into account that people are using, cluely, using chatgpt in a different tab and tab switching people? people using their phone or a second monitor, is that what we're saying it takes into account?
Yes. This solution specifically targets those "invisible" threats by ignoring the software and focusing on the biology.

Here is the breakdown of exactly how this logic catches the specific scenarios you asked about:

1. The "Cluely" User (Overlay Cheating)

The Cheat: They have an invisible overlay on the screen. They see the code, they type it.

Why we catch them: Even though the software is invisible, the human behavior is distinct. A human transcribing text (reading -> typing) has a mathematically "robotic" variance compared to a human thinking (pause... type... delete... pause).

The Signal: High Linearity (no backspaces) + Low Rhythm Variance + Zero Focus Loss (suspicious because normal devs switch tabs to check docs).

2. The "Phone / Second Monitor" User (Air-Gapped)

The Cheat: They read the answer from a phone on their desk or a second monitor.

Why we catch them: The Gaze Cycle.

The human brain has a limited "Working Memory Buffer" for random characters (about 5-9 chunks).

The Cheater must look at the phone (Pause ~1.5s), memorize 10 chars, look at the screen, type them (Burst), then stop to read again.

The Signal: The Oscillation Alert. This creates a "Sinusoidal Wave" of activity that doesn't happen when you are thinking.

3. The "ChatGPT Tab Switcher"

The Cheat: They Alt+Tab to ChatGPT, copy, switch back, and paste (or type).

Why we catch them:

If they paste: The paste event catches a bulk insertion.

If they type: The focus_loss event catches the Alt+Tab.

The Signal: Focus Loss Events.

Day 4 Code: The "Oscillation Engine"

This adds the specific logic to detect that "Read -> Type -> Read -> Type" heartbeat pattern of a phone cheater.

1. Update lib/IntegrityTracker.ts

Replace your current file with this upgraded version. It adds the BurstAnalysis engine.

TypeScript
// lib/IntegrityTracker.ts

export type IntegrityEventType = 

| 'paste' 
| 'focus_loss' 
| 'velocity_spike' 
| 'rhythm_anomaly' 
| 'linearity_alert' 
| 'bulk_insert'
| 'read_pattern_warning'; // New: The "Phone Cheater" signal

interface BurstData {
  length: number;      // Chars typed in this burst
  pauseDuration: number; // Time spent "reading" before this burst
  timestamp: number;
}

export interface TelemetryEvent {
  type: IntegrityEventType;
  timestamp: number;
  data: any;
}

export class IntegrityTracker {
  private buffer: TelemetryEvent =;
  private lastKeyTime: number = 0;
  
  // Welford's Algorithm (Rhythm)
  private count = 0;
  private mean = 0;
  private M2 = 0;

  // Linearity Tracking
  private cursorHistory: { line: number; col: number; time: number } =;
  private linearityWindow = 50;

  // Bulk Insert Tracking
  private lastCharCount = 0;
  private keystrokesSinceContentCheck = 0;

  // Focus Tracking
  private keystrokesSinceLastFocusLoss = 0;

  // --- NEW: Oscillation (Gaze Cycle) Tracking ---
  private currentBurstLength = 0;
  private burstHistory: BurstData =;
  private BURST_PAUSE_THRESHOLD = 1000; // 1 second pause = "I am reading"

  constructor(
    private sessionId: string,
    private onAnomaly: (event: TelemetryEvent) => void
  ) {}

  public trackKeystroke(char: string) {
    const now = Date.now();
    
    if (this.lastKeyTime === 0) {
      this.lastKeyTime = now;
      return;
    }

    const latency = now - this.lastKeyTime;

    // --- 1. Oscillation Logic (Phone Detection) ---
    if (latency > this.BURST_PAUSE_THRESHOLD) {
      // A long pause just happened. The previous burst is over.
      if (this.currentBurstLength > 0) {
        this.recordBurst(this.currentBurstLength, latency);
      }
      this.currentBurstLength = 1; // Start new burst
    } else {
      this.currentBurstLength++;
    }

    // --- 2. Standard Rhythm Logic ---
    if (latency < 30) {
      this.addEvent('velocity_spike', { latency, char });
    }

    this.updateStats(latency);
    this.checkRhythmAnomaly();
    
    // Update counters
    this.lastKeyTime = now;
    this.keystrokesSinceContentCheck++;
    this.keystrokesSinceLastFocusLoss++;
  }

  // --- NEW: Record and Analyze Bursts ---
  private recordBurst(length: number, pauseDuration: number) {
    this.burstHistory.push({ length, pauseDuration, timestamp: Date.now() });
    
    // Analyze last 10 bursts
    if (this.burstHistory.length > 10) {
      this.burstHistory.shift();
      this.analyzeOscillation();
    }
  }

  private analyzeOscillation() {
    if (this.burstHistory.length < 5) return;

    // We look for consistency. Real coders are inconsistent.
    // Transcribers (Phone/Cluely) are consistent.

    // 1. Burst Length Consistency (Buffer Size)
    const lengths = this.burstHistory.map(b => b.length);
    const meanLen = this.calcMean(lengths);
    const cvLen = this.calcStdDev(lengths, meanLen) / meanLen; // Coefficient of Variation

    // 2. Pause Consistency (Reading Speed)
    const pauses = this.burstHistory.map(b => b.pauseDuration);
    const meanPause = this.calcMean(pauses);
    const cvPause = this.calcStdDev(pauses, meanPause) / meanPause;

    // HEURISTIC: 
    // If you type 10-20 chars, pause 2s, type 10-20 chars, pause 2s...
    // consistently (CV < 0.5), you are reading, not thinking.
    
    const isRoboticPattern = cvLen < 0.5 && cvPause < 0.5;
    const isReadingPause = meanPause > 1000 && meanPause < 4000; // 1s - 4s to read
    const isShortBurst = meanLen > 5 && meanLen < 30; // 5-30 chars memory buffer

    if (isRoboticPattern && isReadingPause && isShortBurst) {
      this.addEvent('read_pattern_warning', {
        confidence: 'medium',
        reason: 'Detected rhythmic read/type cycles (Phone/Second Screen pattern)',
        metrics: { meanBurst: meanLen, meanPause: meanPause, consistency: (1 - cvLen).toFixed(2) }
      });
      this.burstHistory =; // Reset to avoid spam
    }
  }

  // --- Helper Math ---
  private calcMean(data: number) {
    return data.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0) / data.length;
  }
  
  private calcStdDev(data: number, mean: number) {
    return Math.sqrt(data.reduce((a, b) => a + Math.pow(b - mean, 2), 0) / data.length);
  }

  // --- Cursor Linearity Logic (Day 3) ---
  public trackCursor(line: number, col: number) {
    const now = Date.now();
    this.cursorHistory.push({ line, col, time: now });
    
    if (this.cursorHistory.length > this.linearityWindow) {
      this.cursorHistory.shift();
      this.analyzeLinearity();
    }
  }

  private analyzeLinearity() {
    let linearMoves = 0;
    for (let i = 1; i < this.cursorHistory.length; i++) {
      const prev = this.cursorHistory[i - 1];
      const curr = this.cursorHistory[i];
      if (curr.line === prev.line && curr.col === prev.col + 1) {
        linearMoves++;
      }
    }
    const linearityIndex = linearMoves / (this.cursorHistory.length - 1);

    // Compound Check: Linearity + Rhythm + NO Focus Loss
    const variance = this.getVariance();
    const isSuspiciousRhythm = variance < 200 && this.mean < 100;
    const noFocusLoss = this.keystrokesSinceLastFocusLoss > 100;

    if (linearityIndex > 0.95 && isSuspiciousRhythm && noFocusLoss) {
      this.addEvent('linearity_alert', { 
        index: linearityIndex.toFixed(2), 
        message: 'High probability of AI transcription (Linear+Rhythmic+NoFocus)' 
      });
      this.cursorHistory =; 
      this.resetStats();
    }
  }

  // --- Bulk Insert (Day 3) ---
  public trackContentChange(newLength: number, changeText: string) {
    const delta = newLength - this.lastCharCount;
    // Check for large insertion that WASN'T a paste event
    if (delta > 10 && this.keystrokesSinceContentCheck < 2) {
       this.addEvent('bulk_insert', { delta, text: changeText.slice(0, 20) });
    }
    this.lastCharCount = newLength;
    this.keystrokesSinceContentCheck = 0;
  }

  // --- Standard Helpers ---
  public trackPaste(length: number) {
    this.addEvent('paste', { length });
  }

  public trackFocus(focused: boolean) {
    if (!focused) {
      this.addEvent('focus_loss', { timestamp: Date.now() });
      this.keystrokesSinceLastFocusLoss = 0; // Reset "Suspicion Counter"
    }
  }

  private updateStats(val: number) {
    this.count++;
    const delta = val - this.mean;
    this.mean += delta / this.count;
    this.M2 += delta * (val - this.mean);
  }

  private getVariance() { return this.count < 2? 0 : this.M2 / (this.count - 1); }

  private checkRhythmAnomaly() {
    if (this.count < 50) return;
    const variance = this.getVariance();
    if (variance < 30) { // Super robotic
      this.addEvent('rhythm_anomaly', { variance, severity: 'critical' });
      this.resetStats();
    }
  }

  private resetStats() {
    this.count = 0; this.mean = 0; this.M2 = 0;
  }

  private addEvent(type: IntegrityEventType, data: any) {
    this.buffer.push({ type, timestamp: Date.now(), data });
    if (type!== 'focus_loss') this.onAnomaly({ type, timestamp: Date.now(), data });
  }

  public flush() {
    const events = [...this.buffer];
    this.buffer =;
    return events;
  }
}
